New Tradeoffs for Decremental Approximate All-Pairs Shortest Paths

Michal Dory, Sebastian Forster, Yasamin Nazari, Tijn de Vos

Abstract

We provide new tradeoffs between approximation and running time for the decremental all-pairs shortest paths (APSP) problem. For undirected graphs with m edges and n nodes undergoing edge deletions, we provide four new approximate decremental APSP algorithms, two for weighted and two for unweighted graphs. Introduction

The dynamic algorithms paradigm is becoming increasingly popular for studying algorithmic questions in the presence of gradually changing inputs. A natural goal in this area is to design algorithms that process each change to the input as fast as possible to adapt the algorithm’s output (or a data structure for querying the output) to the current state of the input. The time spent after an update to perform these computations is called the update time of the algorithm. In many cases, bounds on the update time are obtained in an amortized sense as an average over a long enough sequence of updates. Among dynamic graph problems, the question of maintaining exact or approximate shortest paths has received considerable attention in the past two decades [–]. The main focus usually lies on maintaining a distance oracle that answers queries for the distance between a pair of nodes. For this problem, we call an algorithm fully dynamic if it supports both insertions and deletions of edges, and partially dynamic if it supports only one type of updates; in particular we call it decremental if it only supports edge deletions (which is the focus of this paper), and incremental if it only supports edge insertions.

The running times of partially dynamic algorithms are usually characterized by their bounds on the total update time, which is the accumulated time for processing all updates in a sequence of at most m deletions (where m is the maximum number of edges ever contained the graph). A typical design choice, which we also impose in this paper, is small (say polylogarithmic) query time. In particular, our algorithms will have constant query time. While fully dynamic algorithms are more general, the restriction to only one type of updates in partially dynamic algorithms often admits much faster update times. In particular, some partially dynamic algorithms have a total update time that almost matches the running time of the fastest static algorithm, i.e., computing all updates does not take significantly more time than processing the graph once.

Decremental shortest paths. For the decremental single-source shortest paths problem, conditional lower bounds [, ] suggest that exact decremental algorithms have an Ω (mn)Ω(mn) bottleneck in their total update time (up to subpolynomial factors). On the other hand, this problem admits a (1 + ε ) (1+ε)-approximation (also called stretch) with total update time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$ m^{1 + o(1)} $$\end{document}m1+o(1) in weighted, undirected graphs [, , , , ], which exceeds the running time of the state-of-the art static algorithm by only a subpolynomial factor. Hence for the single-source shortest paths problem on undirected graphs we can fully characterize for which multiplicative stretches the total update time of the fastest decremental algorithm matches (up to subpolynomial factors) the running time of the fastest static algorithm.

Obtaining a similar characterization for the decremental all-pairs shortest paths (APSP) problem is an intriguing open question. Conditional lower bounds [, ] suggest an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Apart from two notable exceptions [, ], all known decremental APSP algorithms fall into one of two categories. They either (a) maintain exact distances or have a relatively small multiplicative stretch of (1 + ε ) (1+ε) or (b) they have a stretch of at least 3. The space in between is largely unexplored in the decremental setting. This stands in sharp contrast to the static setting where for undirected graphs approximation guarantees different from multiplicative (1 + ε ) (1+ε) or “3 and above” have been the focus of a large body of works [, –]. The aforementioned exceptions in dynamic algorithms [, ], concern unweighted undirected graphs and maintain (1 + ε , 2) (1+ε,2)-approximations that simultaneously have a multiplicative error of 1 + ε 1+ε and an additive error of 2, which implies a purely multiplicative (2 + ε ) (2+ε)-approximation. The algorithms run in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Although we are not aware of any lower bounds, it seems to be hard to beat this: no improvements have been made since. The lack of progress in the fully-dynamic setting motivates the study in a partially dynamic setting, where we obtain improvements for the decremental case.

Our Results

In this paper, we provide novel decremental APSP algorithms with approximation guarantees that previously were mostly unexplored in the decremental setting. For an overview of our results, see Table . Our algorithms are randomized and we assume an oblivious adversary, i.e., the sequence of updates is fixed at the start and thus independent of the random choices in the algorithm. For each pair of nodes, we can provide not only the distance estimate, but also a shortest path π π of this length in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(|\pi |)$$\end{document}O~(|π|) time using standard techniques, see e.g. []. In all our algorithms, we maintain an APSP distance oracle, that is, a data structure that answers APSP-queries in constant time, see Sect. for details on the model. In this section, we assume ε ε to be a constant and hence can hide 1/ε 1/ε factors in our asymptotic notation. Update times with ε ε as a parameter, and hence containing 1/ε 1/ε factors, can be found in our technical sections.

(2+ε )(2+ε)-APSP for unweighted graphs. We obtain this result by a general reduction from mixed approximations to purely multiplicative approximations, which might be of independent interest beyond the dynamic setting. See Theorem for the statement. We can then combine this with Theorem to obtain a fast algorithm for non-dense unweighted graphs.

 \begin{document}$$m\le n^{6/5}$$\end{document}m≤n6/5, this approach is faster than our weighted result, Theorem , which was already beating the unweighted state-of-the-art [, ].(1+ε , 2(k-1))(1+ε,2(k-1))-APSP for unweighted graphs. Our fourth contribution is an algorithm for unweighted, undirected graphs that maintains, for any k ≥ 2 k≥2, a (1 + ε 1+ε,2(k-1)2(k-1))-approximation, i.e., a distance estimate that has a multiplicative error of 1 + ε 1+ε and an additive error of 2(k-1) 2(k-1).

Static follow-up work. Interestingly, our techniques inspired a new algorithm for a static 2-approximate distance oracle [], giving the first such distance oracle algorithm with subquadratic construction time for sparse graphs. Moreover, for m=nm=n they match conditional lower bounds [, ].

Related Work

Static algorithms. The baseline for the static APSP problem are the exact textbook algorithms with running times of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

There are several works obtaining improvements upon these running times by either shaving subpolynomial factors or by employing fast matrix multiplication, which sometimes comes at the cost of a (1 + ε ) (1+ε)-approximation instead of an exact result. See [] and [], and the references therein, for details on these approaches. For the regime of stretch 3 and more in undirected graphs, a multitude of algorithms have been developed with the distance oracle of Thorup and Zwick [] arguably being the most well-known construction. In the following, we focus on summarizing the state of affairs for approximate APSP with stretch between 1 + ε 1+ε and 3.

Decremental algorithms. The fastest algorithms for maintaining weighted, exact APSP under edge deletions have total update time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

All of these decremental approximation algorithms are randomized and assume an oblivious adversary. Deterministic algorithms with stretch 1 + ε 1+ε exist for unweighted, undirected graphs with running time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Decremental approximate APSP algorithms of larger stretch, namely at least 3, have first been studied by Baswana, Hariharan, and Sen []. After a series of improvements [, , , ], the state-of-the-art algorithms of [, ] maintain (2k-1)(1 + ε ) (2k-1)(1+ε)-approximate all-pairs shortest paths for any integer k ≥ 2 k≥2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Deterministic decremental algorithms have been developed by Chuzhoy and Saranurak [] and by Chuzhoy [], which have even been improved and generalized to fully dynamic algorithms. For example, Haeupler, Long, and Saranurak[] provide worst update time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}

 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$ \tilde{O} (m^{\mu }) $$\end{document}O~(mμ) for any constant μ μ and constant stretch. As observed by Mądry [], decremental approximate APSP algorithms that are deterministic – or more generally work against an adaptive adversary – can lead to fast static approximation algorithms for the maximum multicommodity flow problem via the Garg-Könemann-Fleischer framework [, ]. Similarly, decremental Single Source Shortest Paths (again, deterministic or against an adaptive adversary) play a role in the recent fast min cost flow algorithms [, , ]. The above upper bounds for decremental APSP have recently been contrasted by conditional lower bounds [] stating that constant stretch cannot be achieved with subpolynomial update and query time under certain hardness assumptions on 3SUM or (static) APSP.

Fully dynamic algorithms. Next, we discuss the related work on fully dynamic shortest paths. We would like to point out that all “combinatorial” algorithms discussed in the following (i.e., algorithms that do not rely on “algebraic” techniques like dynamic matrix inverse) are internally employing decremental algorithms.

The reference point in fully dynamic APSP with subpolynomial query time is the exact algorithm of Demetrescu and Italiano [] with update time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Subsequently, researchers have developed algorithms with subcubic worst-case update time and constant query time [, ] with some of them being deterministic [, ]. It can be made deterministic using the deterministic approximate single-source shortest path algorithm by Bernstein, Probst Gutenberg, and Saranurak []. Further, we note that any algorithm whose update time depends on the sparsity of the graph (possibly also a static one) can be run on a spanner of the input graph maintained by a fully dynamic spanner algorithm []. Finally, recent works [, ] have developed deterministic fully dynamic distance oracles. In particular, the work of Kyng, Meierhans, and Probst Gutenberg [] achieves an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Outline

We give an overview of the notation, terminology, and various decremental tools we use in Sect. . Next, we give a high-level overview of our techniques and provide an intuition for our algorithms in Sect. . Then follow three technical sections. In Sect. , we prove Theorem . Then we prove Theorem and in Sect. . Lastly, we prove Theorem in Sect. .

Preliminaries

Notation and Terminology

Throughout this paper, neighbors of a node v are denoted by N(v). We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

When G is a weighted graph, we allow for positive, integer weights. We denote by W the ratio between the maximum and minimum weight of the graph. Moreover, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

We are interested in designing dynamic graph algorithms for shortest path problems, which support both update and query operations. In particular, we look at decremental algorithms, which support weight increases and edge deletions. This is opposed to fully dynamic algorithms, which also support weight decreases and edge insertions. Bunches and Clusters

We start by reviewing the concepts of bunch and cluster as defined in the seminal distance oracle construction of Thorup and Zwick []. Given a parameter p∈ [0,1]p∈[0,1], called the cluster sampling rate, we let A be a set of sampled nodes, where each node is sampled uniformly at random with probability p. Then we define the pivot of a node v to be the closest node in A, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$p(v)=p_A(v)=\mathop {\mathrm {arg\,min}}\limits _{s\in A} d(s,v)$$\end{document}p(v)=pA(v)=argmins∈Ad(s,v). Since our algorithms contain only one level of sampling, and thus A we will be fixed throughout, we omit the subscript A henceforth. The bunch
B(v) of a node v is the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$B(v):=\{ u \in V: d(u,v) < d(v,p(v)) \}$$\end{document}B(v):={u∈V:d(u,v)<d(v,p(v))} and the clusters are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$C(u):= \{ v \in V: d(u,v) < d(v,p(v)) \}=\{v \in V: u\in B(v)\}$$\end{document}C(u):={v∈V:d(u,v)<d(v,p(v))}={v∈V:u∈B(v)}. Hence bunches and clusters are inverses of each other: u ∈ B(v)u∈B(v) if and only if v ∈ C(u)v∈C(u).

Thorup and Zwick showed how to compute bunches and clusters in the static setting in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\tfrac{m}{p})$$\end{document}O~(mp) time []. However, it is challenging to maintain them exactly under deletions: for starters, we cannot compute exact distances from the pivots efficiently. In the static setting, we can compute such distances in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(pnm)$$\end{document}O~(pnm) time, by running Dijkstra’s algorithm from every pivot. In the decremental setting, we only have efficient exact shortest path computation (through ES-trees, defined in Sect. ) when we restrict to bounded depth, which is not sufficient for our applications. It is possible to maintain approximate bunches that can deal with these challenges. We note that Roditty and Zwick [] provide an algorithm that is efficient for maintaining decremental bunches exactly, but again only up to bounded depth, see Lemma . So, this is too expensive for our purpose. Instead we use another type of approximate bunches proposed by Łącki and Nazari []. We start by relaxing the definition of the pivot: the approximate pivot \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{p}(v)\in A$$\end{document}p~(v)∈A of a node v does not have to be the closest node in A, but just within a (1+ε )(1+ε) factor of it:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} d_G(v,p(v)) \le \delta _{\tilde{B}}(v,\tilde{p}(v)) \le (1+\epsilon ) d_G(v,p(v)). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dG(v,p(v))≤δB~(v,p~(v))≤(1+ε)dG(v,p(v)).Next, we define an approximate bunch \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{B}(v)$$\end{document}B~(v), which rather than containing the exact bunch B(v) contains all nodes that are at least a factor 1/(1+ε )1/(1+ε) closer than the exact pivot p(v). More precisely, for our analysis, our approximate bunch \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{B}(v)$$\end{document}B~(v) needs to contain all nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \usepackage{amssymb}

 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\{ w \in V: (1+\epsilon )d_G(v,w) < d_G(v, p(v)) \}$$\end{document}{w∈V:(1+ε)dG(v,w)<dG(v,p(v))}. More precisely, in the stretch analysis of Theorem (see Lemma ), we need that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}

 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$d_G(v,w) \ge \tfrac{d_G(v,p(v))}{1+\epsilon }$$\end{document}dG(v,w)≥dG(v,p(v))1+ε, i.e., that nodes not in the bunch are far enough away. Since we can lose a factor (1+ε )(1+ε) in our distance estimates, we do not need that the nodes are actually farther away than the pivot (this would mean that the entire bunch \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$B(v)\subseteq \tilde{B}(v)$$\end{document}B(v)⊆B~(v)), but it suffices that all nodes sufficiently closer than the pivot are in there. It can also contain additional nodes. For efficiency purposes, we still want the approximate bunch to remain small, say only polylogarithmically larger than the exact bunch B(v).

Finally, for every node v∈ Vv∈V and every node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \begin{document}$$\delta _{\tilde{B}}(u,v)$$\end{document}δB~(u,v). Here also an approximate distance will suffice:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

The lemma outputs: approximate pivots, approximate bunches, and a distance estimate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Lemma 2.1

 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}

 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}

ES-Trees

An Even-Shiloach tree (ES-tree) is a central data structure for maintaining distances in a decremental setting. It maintains the exact distances from a source s under edge deletions, giving the following.

Lemma 2.3

([–]) There is a data structure called ES-tree that, given an integer-weighted directed graph G undergoing deletions and edge weight increases, a root node s, and a depth parameter d, maintains, for every node v a value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

 \begin{document}$$\ell _s(v) = d_G(s,v)$$\end{document}ls(v)=dG(s,v) if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}

 \begin{document}$$d_G(s,v) \le d$$\end{document}dG(s,v)≤d and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

A monotone ES-tree is a generalization of ES-tree studied in [, , , ] that handles edge insertions. This is a data structure that maintains distances from a source s in a graph undergoing deletions, insertions and edge weight increases. Note that when edges are inserted to the graph, it can potentially decrease some distances in the graph. However, the data structure ignores such decreases, and maintains distance estimates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\ell _s(v)$$\end{document}ls(v) from s to any other node, that can only increase during the algorithm. In particular, these distance estimates are not necessarily the correct distances in the graph, and when using this data structure the goal is to prove that these estimates still provide a good approximation for the current distances. We call \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\ell _s(v)$$\end{document}ls(v) the level of v in the monotone ES-tree rooted at s.

High-Level Overview

In this section we provide a high-level overview of our algorithms. First we describe our (2+ε )(2+ε)-APSP algorithms for weighted graphs, by giving a simpler static version first. Then we describe our \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$(2+\epsilon ,W_{u,v})$$\end{document}(2+ε,Wu,v)-APSP algorithm, where we introduce a notion of bunch overlap threshold to overcome the challenge of dynamically maintaining a well-known static adaptive sampling technique [] (see Sect. ). We give a reduction from mixed approximations to purely multiplicative approximations, which together with the previous result gives our (2+ε )(2+ε)-APSP algorithm for unweighted graphs. Finally, we describe our (1+ε , 2(k-1))(1+ε,2(k-1))-APSP algorithm for any k ≥ 2k≥2 in unweighted graphs.

As in Sect. , we assume ε ε to be a constant in this section. Hence, we can hide 1/ε 1/ε factors in our asymptotic notation. Update times with ε ε as a parameter, and hence containing 1/ε 1/ε factors, can be found in our technical sections. In all cases, these factors are inherited from the use of decremental single source shortest path (SSSP) algorithms (see, e.g., []), and hence do not form a critical part of our paper.

(2+ε )(2+ε)-APSP for Weighted Graphs

We first present a warm-up static algorithm that we later turn into a dynamic algorithm.

Static 2-APSP

We consider the bunches and clusters of the well-known distance oracles of Thorup and Zwick [], as introduced in Sect. . In particular, we consider sampling probability p, set of sampled nodes A, and bunches B(v) and clusters C(v) for v∈ Vv∈V. We store the distances A× VA×V, together with the clusters and bunches of each node. Moreover, we store the distances d(u, v) for all u∈ Vu∈V and v∈ B(u)v∈B(u). With this information, we can query 3-approximate distances for any pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$u,v\in V\times V$$\end{document}u,v∈V×V. At a high-level, this works as follows. We either have v ∈ B(u)v∈B(u) and so we explicitly have stored their distance, or we can get a 3-approximate estimate by computing d(u,p(u))+d(p(u),v)d(u,p(u))+d(p(u),v), since\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} d(u,p(u))+d(p(u),v) \le d(u,p(u))+d(u,p(u))+d(u,v) \le 3d(u,v), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}d(u,p(u))+d(p(u),v)≤d(u,p(u))+d(u,p(u))+d(u,v)≤3d(u,v),where the first step follows by the triangle inequality and the second step by the fact that d(u,p(u)) ≤ d(u,v)d(u,p(u))≤d(u,v) if v∉ B(u)v∉B(u). In the following, we explain that in some special cases we can directly use the estimate d(u,p(u))+d(p(u),v)d(u,p(u))+d(p(u),v) to obtain a 2-approximation for a pair u, v. In other cases, we can store more information depending on how B(u) and B(v) overlap and use this to obtain a 2-approximation to d(u, v).

Let us assume that for all nodes v we have computed B(v) and have access to all the distances from v to each node u ∈ B(v)u∈B(v). Also assume that we have computed the distances from the set A to all nodes. We now describe how using this information we can get a 2-approximate estimate of any pair of nodes u, v in the first case. In another case we will discuss what other estimates we need to precompute. Let π π be a shortest path between u and v. We consider two cases depending on how the bunches B(u) and B(v) interact with π π. A similar case analysis was used in [, ] in distributed approximate shortest paths algorithms. There exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$w \in \pi $$\end{document}w∈π such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$w \not \in B(u) \cup B(v)$$\end{document}w∉B(u)∪B(v) (left case in Fig. ): Since w is on the shortest path π π, we either have d(w,u) ≤ d(u,v)/2d(w,u)≤d(u,v)/2 or d (v,w) ≤ d(u,v)/2d(v,w)≤d(u,v)/2. Suppose d(w,u) ≤ d(u,v)/2d(w,u)≤d(u,v)/2. We observe that by definition d(u,p(u))≤ d(u,w)d(u,p(u))≤d(u,w), hence we obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$d(u,p(u))+d(p(u),v)\le d(u,p(u))+d(u,p(u))+d(u,v) \le 2d(u,v)$$\end{document}d(u,p(u))+d(p(u),v)≤d(u,p(u))+d(u,p(u))+d(u,v)≤2d(u,v), where the first step follows by the triangle inequality and the second step by the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}

There are several subtleties that make it more difficult to maintain these estimates in decremental settings. Not only do distances change, but also which nodes are part of a bunch change due to the updates. Moreover, which bunches are adjacent to each other changes. We next discuss how these intricacies can be handled. We obtain a total update time that matches the stated static running time (up to subpolynomial factors).

Dynamic Challenges

Maintaining bunches and pivots. First, we need to dynamically maintain bunches efficiently as nodes may join and leave a bunch throughout the updates using an adaptation of prior work. One option would be maintaining the clusters and bunches using the [] framework, however using this directly is slow for our purposes. Hence we maintain approximate clusters and bunches using hopsets of Łącki and Nazari []. This algorithm also maintains approximate pivots, i.e. pivots that are within (1+ε )(1+ε)-approximate distance of the true closest sampled node. These estimates let us handle the first case (up to (1+ε )(1+ε) approximation). One subtlety is that the type of approximate bunches used in [] is slightly different from the type of approximate bunches we need for other parts of our algorithm. Specifically, we need to bound the number of times nodes in a bunch can change. Roughly speaking, we can show this since the graph is decremental and the estimates obtained from the algorithm of [] are monotone. Hence we perform lazy bunch updates, where we only let a bunch grow if the distance to the pivot grows by at least a factor 1+ε 1+ε. This means a bunch grows at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$O(\log _{1+\epsilon }(nW))$$\end{document}O(log1+ε(nW)) times. In addition, these approximate bunches need to be taken into account in our stretch analysis.

We note that (1+ε )(1+ε)-approximate decremental SSSP takes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(m^{1+o(1)})$$\end{document}O~(m1+o(1)) total update time. However, we use the multi-source shortest path result from [], where the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$n^{o(1)}$$\end{document}no(1)-term vanishes when applied with polynomially many sources, giving us a total update time of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(|A|m)$$\end{document}O~(|A|m).

Maintaining estimates for Case 2. When we are in Case 2, we need more tools to keep track of estimates going through these nodes since both the bunches and the distances involved are changing. In particular, we explain how by using heaps in different parts of our algorithm we get efficient update and query times. Moreover, note that we have an additional complication: there are two data structures in this step, where one impacts the other. We need to ensure that an update in the original graph does not lead to many changes in the first data structure, which all need to be processed for the second data structure.

To be precise, we need intermediate data structures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \usepackage{upgreek}

However, this approach has a problem: δ (v',v)δ(v′,v) might change many times, and for each such change we need to update at most |N(v')|≤ n|N(v′)|≤n heaps. To overcome this problem, we use another notion of lazy distance update: we only update a bunch if the estimate of a node changes by at least a factor 1+ε 1+ε. Combining this with the fact that, due to the lazy bunch update, nodes only join a cluster at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Similarly, w(u',v')w(u′,v′) might change many times, which has an impact for every v∈ C(v')v∈C(v′). Again the solution is a lazy update scheme: instead of w(u',v')w(u′,v′) we use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

We note that our bunches are approximate bunches in three different ways. We have one notion of approximation due to the fact that we are using hopsets (that implicitly maintain bunches on scaled graphs). We have a second notion of approximation due to our lazy bunch update, which only lets nodes join a bunch a bounded number of times. We have a third notion of approximation due to the lazy distance update, which only propagates distance changes O(łog (nW))O(log(nW)) times. We need to carefully consider how these different notions of approximate bunch interact with each other and with the stretch of the algorithm.

 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(nC_{\max }/p)$$\end{document}O~(nCmax/p) time, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$C_{\max }$$\end{document}Cmax is the size of the largest cluster. First we focus on the challenge of bounding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$C_{\max }$$\end{document}Cmax.

Efficiency challenge. While the bunches are small, there is no bound on the size of the clusters. To overcome this issue in the static case, Thorup and Zwick developed an alternative way to build the bunches and clusters, that guarantees that the clusters are also small []. At a high-level, their approach is to adaptively change (grow) A by sampling big clusters into smaller clusters using a sampling rate proportional to the cluster size and adding these nodes to the set of pivots A. In total, in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(m/p)$$\end{document}O~(m/p) time we obtain bunches and clusters, both of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(1/p)$$\end{document}O~(1/p). This idea was developed in the context of obtaining compact routing schemes, and later found numerous applications in static algorithms for approximating shortest paths and distances (see e.g., [, , , ]).

Using this trick, the static algorithm has running time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\tfrac{m}{p}+pnm+\tfrac{n}{p^2})=\tilde{O}(nm^{3/4}+m^{4/3})$$\end{document}O~(mp+pnm+np2)=O~(nm3/4+m4/3), for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$p=m^{-1/3}$$\end{document}p=m-1/3 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(mn^{1/2}+n^2)$$\end{document}O~(mn1/2+n2) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$p=n^{-1/2}$$\end{document}p=n-1/2.

However, the adaptive sampling procedure does not seem to be well-suited to a dynamic setting. The reason is that the sampling procedure is adaptive in such a way that when the bunches/clusters change, the set of sampled nodes can also change. Although (a dynamic adaptation of) the algorithm can guarantee that the set of sampled nodes is small at any particular moment, this would require to both add nodes to and remove nodes from A at any time. This would add a significant amount of computation necessary to propagate these changes to other parts of the algorithm. A possible solution is to enforce A to only grow, by never letting nodes leave A. This would require us to bound the total number of nodes that would ever be sampled, which seems impossible with the current approach. In that approach, we could only bound the number of new nodes for a single deletion, which can become too larger after many deletions. To overcome this issue, we next suggest a new approach that gives us a set that is only increasing in size, but remains bounded. For simplicity, we first present this in a static setting.

Introducing bunch overlap thresholds. Our proposed approach is to divide the nodes into two types depending on the number of bunches they appear in. In particular, we consider a threshold parameter τ τ and we define a node to be a heavy node if it is in more than τ τ bunches (equivalently its cluster contains more than τ τ nodes), and otherwise we call it a light node. In other words, for the set of light nodes we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$C_{\max }\le \tau $$\end{document}Cmax≤τ. This threshold introduces a second type of ‘pivot’ for each node u, which we denote by q(u) that is defined to be the closest heavy node to u. In particular, instead of computing all distance estimates going through nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$w \in B(u) \cap B(v)$$\end{document}w∈B(u)∩B(v), we only compute them for the case that w is light, and otherwise it is enough to compute the minimum estimates going through the closest heavy node, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\min \{ d(u,q(u))+d(q(u),v), d(v,q(v))+d(q(v),u)\}$$\end{document}min{d(u,q(u))+d(q(u),v),d(v,q(v))+d(q(v),u)}. We emphasize that although the heavy nodes fullfill a similar role to the pivots, they are not a subset of our pivots p(v), v ∈ Vp(v),v∈V. Moreover, heavy nodes have a different behavior in bounding the running time than the pivots. As we will see later, introducing these thresholds is crucial in efficiently obtaining the estimates required for Case 2 as we can handle the light and heavy case separately.

Stretch of the algorithm with bunch overlap thresholds. As discussed above, the stretch analysis was divided into two cases. In the new variant of the algorithm we add a third case, where we look at the distance estimates going through the closest heavy node, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\min \{ d(u,q(u))+d(q(u),v), d(v,q(v))+d(q(v),u)\}$$\end{document}min{d(u,q(u))+d(q(u),v),d(v,q(v))+d(q(v),u)}. The main idea is that now we only need to consider the difficult case, Case 2, if the relevant node is light, which allows us to implement the algorithm efficiently.

Again, for a decremental algorithm, we need to combine this new idea with the subtleties we had in the previous section: the bunches, their overlaps, and the heavy nodes keep on changing over the updates. We obtain a total update time that matches the stated static running time (up to subpolynomial factors) for the algorithm using bunch overlap thresholds.

(2+ε )(2+ε)-APSP for Unweighted Graphs

We observe that in unweighted graphs mixed approximation can always be turned into a multiplicative approximation – at the cost of a blow-up in the number of vertices. More precisely, we prove the following reduction.

Theorem 3.1

(1+ε , 2(k-1))(1+ε,2(k-1))-APSP for Unweighted Graphs

In this section, we describe our near-additive APSP algorithm. Our work is inspired by a classic result of Dor, Halperin, and Zwick [], that presented a static algorithm that computes purely additive +2(k-1)+2(k-1) approximation for APSP in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}

Dynamic data structure. The static algorithm computes distances in two steps. First, it computes distances from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1 by computing BFS trees in the graph G. This step can be maintained dynamically by using Even-Shiloach trees (ES-trees) [], a data structure that maintains distances in a decremental graph. Maintaining the distances up to distance O(d) takes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$O(|A_1|md)$$\end{document}O(|A1|md) time. The more challenging part is computing distances from the remaining nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}

 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$V \setminus A_1$$\end{document}V\A1. Here the static algorithm computes distances in the graphs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}

 \usepackage{upgreek}

 \usepackage{amsmath}

 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$E_2$$\end{document}E2 is the set of light edges. To keep the correctness of the dynamic algorithm, every time that a node v no longer has a neighbor in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$E^*$$\end{document}E∗ is deleted from G, we should replace it by an alternative edge if such an edge exists. Finally, the weights of the edges in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}

 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$E^{A_1}_u$$\end{document}Eu1 can change over time, when the estimates change. This means that other than deletions of edges, we can also add new edges to the graphs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$H_u$$\end{document}Hu or change the weights of their edges. Because of these edge insertions we can no longer use the standard ES-tree data structure to maintain the distances, because that only works in a decremental setting.

To overcome this, we use monotone ES-trees, a generalization of ES-trees proposed by [, ]. In this data structure, to keep the algorithm efficient, when new edges are inserted, the distance estimates do not change. In particular, when edges are inserted, some distances may decrease, however, in such cases the data structure keeps an old larger estimate of the distance. The main challenge in using this data structure is to show that the stretch analysis still holds. In particular, in our case, the stretch analysis of the static algorithm was based on the fact that distances from the nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$V \setminus A_1$$\end{document}V\A1 are computed in the graphs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$H_u$$\end{document}Hu, where in the dynamic setting, we do not have this guarantee anymore.

Handling large distances. The algorithm described above is efficient if d is small, to obtain an efficient algorithm for the general case, we combine our approach with a decremental near-additive APSP algorithm. More concretely, we use an algorithm from Henzinger et al. [] that computes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Limitations of previous approaches

We next explain why approaches used in previous decremental algorithms cannot be generalized to obtain our near-additive results. First, the unweighted (1+ε ,2)(1+ε,2)-approximate APSP algorithm that takes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{5/2}/\epsilon )$$\end{document}O~(n5/2/ε) time [], is inspired by static algorithms for +2+2-additive emulators, sparse graphs that preserve the distances up to a +2+2-additive stretch. The main idea is to maintain a sparse emulator of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$m' =\tilde{O}(n^{3/2})$$\end{document}m′=O~(n3/2), and exploit its sparsity to obtain a fast algorithm taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(m'n)= \tilde{O}(n^{5/2}/\epsilon )$$\end{document}O~(m′n)=O~(n5/2/ε) time. This construction however is specific for the +2+2 case, and cannot be generalized to a general k. Note that the update time of the algorithm crucially depends on the size of the emulator, and to obtain a better algorithm for a general k, we need to be able to construct a sparser emulator for a general k. However, a beautiful lower bound result by Abboud and Bodwin [] shows that for any constant k and δ >0δ>0, a purely additive +k+k emulator should have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\Omega (n^{4/3-\delta })$$\end{document}Ω(n4/3-δ) edges. This implies that dynamic algorithms that are based on purely additive emulators with m'm′ edges seem to require \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\Theta (n\cdot m')=\Omega (n^{7/3-\delta })$$\end{document}Θ(n·m′)=Ω(n7/3-δ) time, and we cannot get running time arbitrarily close to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$O(n^2)$$\end{document}O(n2). An alternative approach is to build a near-additive emulator. This is done in [], where the authors show a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$(1+\epsilon , 2(1+2/\epsilon )^{k-2})$$\end{document}(1+ε,2(1+2/ε)k-2)-approximation algorithm for decremental APSP with expected total update time of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{2+1/k} (37/\epsilon )^{k-1})$$\end{document}O~(n2+1/k(37/ε)k-1). Here the running time indeed gets closer to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$O(n^2)$$\end{document}O(n2), but this comes at a price of a much worse additive term of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$2(1+2/\epsilon )^{k-2}$$\end{document}2(1+2/ε)k-2. While in the static setting there are also other bounds obtained for near-additive emulators, such as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$O(1+\epsilon ,O(k/\epsilon )^{k-1})$$\end{document}O(1+ε,O(k/ε)k-1) emulators of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$O(kn^{1+\frac{1}{2^{k+1}-1}})$$\end{document}O(kn1+12k+1-1) [–], in all of the constructions the additive term depends on ε ε, and the dependence on ε ε is known to be nearly tight for small k by lower bounds from []. Hence this approach cannot lead to small constant additive terms that do not depend on ε ε.

(2+ε )(2+ε)-Approximate APSP for Weighted Graphs

Given an undirected, weighted graph G=(V,E,w)G=(V,E,w), where w takes positive, integer values, our goal is to maintain (2+ε )(2+ε)-approximate all-pairs distances in G, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\hat{d}$$\end{document}d^. We provide an algorithm with parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$ 0< p,\epsilon \le 1 $$\end{document}0<p,ε≤1, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$0< \rho \le 1$$\end{document}0<ρ≤1 that gives a (2+ε )(2+ε)-approximation of APSP for weighted graphs, with ratio between the maximum and minimum weight W. Overall, the total update time scales with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$pnm + \tfrac{n^2}{p}$$\end{document}pnm+n2p, for an exact statement see Lemma , which explicitly states the dependency on ρ ρ, W, and 1/ε 1/ε. The algorithm has a constant query time.

We recommend the reader to first read Sect. , where we provide a static version of the algorithm and we discuss the challenges and techniques of making this dynamic. This section starts with our high-level dynamic algorithm in Sect. , we provide further details and parametrized update time analysis of each step in Sect. , and we provide the stretch analysis in Sect. . In Sect. , we combine these and set the parameter p appropriately to obtain our final result.

Decremental Data Structures

We will make use of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}

 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$(1+\epsilon _1)$$\end{document}(1+ε1)-approximate bunches and clusters that are introduced in Sect. . However, for efficiency purposes, we do not use these bunches themselves, but instead look at bunches with some additional approximation. The goal is to ensure that the bunches do not increase too often.

Now, we give a description of the algorithm in Algorithm 2, which is a dynamic version of the static algorithm given in Sect. . Next, we give a proof of correctness and show how to implement each of the steps. We provide a separate initialization procedure, as for some steps it will turn out that the initialization step and the update steps are quite different.

Further Details and Time Analysis

In this section, we add further details of the procedures described and analyze the total update time. Considering the sampling of A in the algorithm, we note that A remains unchanged throughout the algorithm, hence it is only sampled once at the initialization. This takes O(n) time. To maintain distances from A, we use the decremental shortest path algorithm from Lemma together with Lemma , which bounds the size of A, to obtain the following lemma.

Query time. Finally, we observe that the query is relatively straightforward and can be done in constant time.

Lemma 4.6

has constant query time.

Proof

A query \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\texttt {Query}(u,v)$$\end{document}Query(u,v) consists of the minimum over four values. The first two can be computed in constant time, as the components are all maintained explicitly. The last two are maintained explicitly. This gives us constant query time in total. □ □

Stretch Analysis

In this section, we show that an adapted version of the algorithm from Sect. returns \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}

 \usepackage{amsmath}

 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$W_{u,v}$$\end{document}Wu,v is the maximum weight on a shortest path from u to v. This result follows by the fact that in this case we do not need to handle the adjacent bunch case, but instead can consider an overlap bunch case (see the middle case in Fig. ), which leads to an improved total update time.

To make this efficient, there are some additional complications. We introduce a parameter τ τ, the bunch overlap threshold, which bounds the cluster size. For nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$w\in B(u)\cap B(v)$$\end{document}w∈B(u)∩B(v) with cluster smaller than τ τ, we can compute the estimate in the overlap case efficiently. If all nodes in the overlap B(u)∩ B(v)B(u)∩B(v) have larger clusters, we obtain an estimate by computing shortest paths from the heavy nodes.

We recommend the reader to first read the static version of this algorithm in Sect. . We then present the details of the dynamic version in Sect. . Moreover, in Sect. , we show our general reduction from mixed APSP to purely multiplicative APSP for unweighted graphs. We use this reduction to obtain our unweighted (2+ε ,0)(2+ε,0)-APSP result.

Theorem
Given a graph
G
with positive, integer weights and a constant
0< ε < 10<ε<1, there is a decremental data structure that maintains a
\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$(2+\epsilon ,W_{u,v})$$\end{document}(2+ε,Wu,v)-approximation for APSP, where
\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$W_{u,v}$$\end{document}Wu,v
is the maximum weight on a current shortest path from
u
to
v. The algorithm has constant query time and works against an oblivious adversary. The total update time is w.h.p. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}( nm^{3/4}\log ^3(nW))$$\end{document}O~(nm3/4log3(nW))
if

 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}

 \begin{document}$$m=n^{1+\Omega (1)}$$\end{document}m=n1+Ω(1), and

 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \usepackage{amssymb}

 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}( n^{1+o(1)}m^{3/4}\log ^3(nW))$$\end{document}O~(n1+o(1)m3/4log3(nW))
otherwise, where
W
is the ratio between the maximum and minimum weight.

A query \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\texttt {Query}(u,v)$$\end{document}Query(u,v) consists of the minimum over three values. The first two can be computed in constant time, as the components are all maintained explicitly. The last one is maintained explicitly. This gives us constant query time in total. □ □

In particular, this means we get (2+ε ,1)(2+ε,1)-approximate APSP for unweighted graphs.Fig. 3Possible scenario for the overlap between the bunches B(u) and B(v) and the shortest path π π from u to v where the estimate can be obtained using the closest heavy node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Reduction and Unweighted (2+ε )(2+ε)-APSP

Next, we provide a reduction that transforms (a+ε ,k)(a+ε,k)-APSP to (a+(k+2)ε ,0)(a+(k+2)ε,0)-APSP on unweighted graphs. This reductions allows for ε =0ε=0 as well. However, for our applications the update time of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

Intuitively, we split every edge into k+1k+1 edges to form G'G′. Then we compute (2, k)-APSP on G'G′, divide the result by k+1k+1, and round down to the nearest integer. Because the additive error k is not enough to reach a new node in G, we can simply ignore this part of the path. In the following theorem we make this formal.

Approximate APSP with Additive Factors

In this section we give our last result, which is maintaining approximate APSP on unweighted graphs with a mixed additive and multiplicative stretch. First, we provide a decremental (1+ε , 2)(1+ε,2)-APSP algorithm as a warm-up in Sect. . In Sect. , we generalize our techniques to provide an efficient decremental (1+ε , 2(k-1))(1+ε,2(k-1))-APSP algorithm.

Claim 6.6

The query time of the algorithm is O(1).

Proof

To answer a distance query for a pair of nodes u, v, we just return the value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}

 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\ell _u(v)$$\end{document}lu(v). This can be done in O(1) time if we keep for each node u an array of the values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\ell _u(v)$$\end{document}lu(v) which is updated each time a distance estimate change. □ □

In conclusion, we get the following.

Theorem 6.7

Given an undirected unweighted graph G, there is a decremental data structure that maintains +2+2-additive approximation for APSP for pairs of nodes at distance at most d from each other. The expected total update time is bounded by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(m^{1/2}n^{3/2}d)$$\end{document}O~(m1/2n3/2d), and the query time is constant.

To get rid of the d term in the complexity, we can combine our approach with Theorem . This would lead to a (1+ε ,2)(1+ε,2)-approximation and replace the d term in the complexity by an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

 \begin{document}$$O(1/\epsilon )^{O(\sqrt{\log {n}})}$$\end{document}O(1/ε)O(logn) term (which simplifies to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$n^{o(1)}$$\end{document}no(1) for constant ε ε. We will provide full details of the approach when we describe our general algorithm in the next section (see in particular Sect. ). The high-level idea is to use our algorithm with a parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

For an appropriate choice of the parameters, we will get a (1+ε )(1+ε)-approximation for the distances between pairs of nodes at distance greater than d from each other, and a +2+2 approximation for pairs of nodes at distance at most d from each other.

(1+ε , 2k-2)(1+ε,2k-2)-APSP

With this warm-up we can move on to the more general algorithm based on [] that leads to a larger stretch but a smaller update time. Our goal is to obtain a (1+ε , 2k-2)(1+ε,2k-2)-approximation in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{2-1/k}m^{1/k}(1/\varepsilon )^{O(\sqrt{\log n})})$$\end{document}O~(n2-1/km1/k(1/ε)O(logn)) time. The algorithm works in unweighted undirected graphs. As before, we start by describing a data structure that maintains the approximate distances for pairs of nodes at distance at most d from each other, and then in Sect. we combine it with Theorem to handle the general case.

Maintaining the monotone ES-trees. The main challenge in the algorithm is maintaining the monotone ES-trees in Step 5 of the algorithm. Before analyzing the time complexity of the algorithm, we start by describing the algorithm in more detail. Note that each time an edge is deleted it can lead to many changes, including edge insertions and weight changes in all the trees we maintain. We next explain in detail in which order we make these changes in order to maintain the correctness of the algorithm. We use the algorithms for ES trees and monotone ES trees described in Sect. .

 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\ell _u(v)$$\end{document}lu(v), and update the value in constant time each time it was changed in the algorithm. □ □

Putting Everything Together

In the last sections we showed an algorithm that for any pair of nodes at distance at most d maintains a +2(k-1)+2(k-1)-additive approximation in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

 \usepackage{wasysym}

 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{2-1/k}m^{1/k}d)$$\end{document}O~(n2-1/km1/kd) time. To get our final result, we combine it with Theorem to handle large distances, proving the following.

Conclusion

We presented new tradeoffs for decremental approximate APSP in undirected graphs. For weighted graphs, we obtained a (2+ɛ )(2+ε)-approximation with total update time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\widetilde{O}(m^{1/2}n^{3/2})$$\end{document}O~(m1/2n3/2) and a faster \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$(2+\varepsilon ,W_{u,v})$$\end{document}(2+ε,Wu,v)-approximation with total update time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\widetilde{O}(nm^{3/4})$$\end{document}O~(nm3/4). For unweighted graphs, we obtained a (2+ɛ )(2+ε)-approximation with total update time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\widetilde{O}(m^{7/4})$$\end{document}O~(m7/4), yielding the first subquadratic algorithm for sufficiently sparse graphs, as well as a family of (1+ɛ ,2(k-1))(1+ε,2(k-1))-approximation algorithms with total update time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\widetilde{O}(n^{2-1/k}m^{1/k})$$\end{document}O~(n2-1/km1/k). These results demonstrate that approximation guarantees between 1+ɛ 1+ε and 3, which have been extensively studied in the static setting [–, , , , ], can also be exploited to obtain substantially faster decremental APSP algorithms.

Despite this progress, the landscape of decremental APSP remains far from fully understood. One of the central open questions is to characterize for which approximation guarantees the total update time of a decremental APSP algorithm can match, up to subpolynomial factors, the running time of the best corresponding static algorithm. For single-source shortest paths, a remarkably complete picture has emerged through a long line of work culminating in near-linear update times for a wide range of approximation guarantees [, , , , , , ]. In contrast, for APSP there remain substantial gaps. For example, our (2+ɛ )(2+ε)-approximate weighted algorithm achieves total update time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\widetilde{O}(m^{1/2}n^{3/2})$$\end{document}O~(m1/2n3/2), matching the classical static construction of Cohen and Zwick [] up to polylogarithmic factors. However, since then faster static 2-approximations have been developed: Baswana and Kavitha [] give an algorithm for 2-APSP taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(m\sqrt{n}+n^2)$$\end{document}O~(mn+n2) time. For dense graphs, Dory et al. [] give (2+ε )(2+ε)-APSP in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$O(n^{2.214})$$\end{document}O(n2.214) time and a distance oracle for 2-APSP taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(mn^{2/3})$$\end{document}O~(mn2/3) time. Determining whether these gaps between decremental and static can be closed, or whether they reflect inherent barriers, remains an open question.

A second major direction concerns robustness against stronger adversaries and deterministic computation. Most of the fastest decremental APSP algorithms, including ours, rely on randomization and are analyzed against an oblivious adversary [, , ]. On the other hand, substantial progress has been made in the deterministic setting for large-stretch approximations and related shortest-path problems [–, , , , , ].

Nevertheless, no deterministic or adaptive-adversary decremental APSP algorithms are known with update times comparable to those achieved in the oblivious-adversary setting for approximation guarantees around 2+ɛ 2+ε. Closing these gaps would represent a major advance in dynamic graph algorithms.

Finally, the fully dynamic setting remains largely unexplored in the approximation regime considered in this paper. Fully dynamic APSP has a long history, beginning with exact algorithms [, , , , ] and continuing with approximate algorithms [, , ]. However, the best known fully dynamic (2+ɛ )(2+ε)-approximate APSP algorithms still require roughly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$m^{1+o(1)}$$\end{document}m1+o(1) amortized update time per operation [], and there has been comparatively little progress toward achieving the kinds of improvements that are now known in the decremental setting. It remains open whether approximation guarantees between 2 and 3 admit substantially faster fully dynamic algorithms and whether techniques such as approximate bunches or hopsets can be extended to support insertions efficiently.

We hope that the techniques introduced in this work—particularly the dynamic maintenance of approximate bunches and the use of bunch-overlap thresholds—provide useful tools for addressing these questions and for further narrowing the gap between static, decremental, and fully dynamic shortest-path algorithms.

Monotone ES-tree

Maintaining Bunches and Clusters

In this section we give a sketch of the algorithm of Łącki and Nazari [] that allows us to maintain bunches and clusters needed for the decremental algorithm in Sect. . One tool used is a decremental algorithm by Roditty and Zwick [] that maintains Thorup-Zwick clusters up to a bounded depth. This result can be summarized as follows:

Lemma B.1

([]). Let 0<p≤ 10<p≤1 and d≥ 1d≥1 be parameters. Given a dynamic graph G=(V,E,w)G=(V,E,w), that has edge deletions, weight increases, and edge insertions that do not decrease distances, we can maintain clusters (with sampling probability p) and bunches up to depth d in total update time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
 \usepackage{amsmath}
 \usepackage{wasysym}
 \usepackage{amsfonts}
 \usepackage{amssymb}
 \usepackage{amsbsy}
 \usepackage{mathrsfs}
 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
 \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(|E(G)|d/p)$$\end{document}O~(|E(G)|d/p). Moreover, we maintain distances from each node to its cluster and its bunch, and these distances are monotone (non-decreasing).

This time dependence on d is too expensive for many applications, and thus [] improve the time for maintaining approximate bunches and clusters by maintaining a hopset, combined with a well-known scaling tool.

Hopsets. Hopsets, originally introduced by Cohen and Zwick [] in the context of parallel algorithms, are widely used in faster algorithms in decremental settings. We briefly define this concept and sketch its application.

Definition 2

Let G=(V,E,w)G=(V,E,w) be a weighted undirected graph, and let 0<ε <10<ε<1 be a parameter. A \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

 \usepackage{upgreek}
 \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}

Scaling. The following scaling technique of Klein and Subramanian [] changes a bounded hop distance problem into a bounded depth distance problem on a scaled graph. Similar ideas have been widely used in dynamic algorithms, see e.g., [, , , ]. As we will see, the bounded depth works well together with the cluster and bunch maintaining algorithm of Roditty and Zwick []

Lemma B.3

([]) Let G=(V,E,w)G=(V,E,w) be a weighted undirected graph. Let R ≥ 0R≥0, d ≥ 1d≥1, ε > 0ε>0 be parameters. We define a scaled graph to be a graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

The final hopsets of [] are the union of all these restricted hopsetes, however for maintaining distances efficiently we need to run the algorithm in Lemma on all these scaled graphs simultaneously and take the minimum estimate over all the scales. Importantly, to handle edge insertions they use monotone ES trees which implies that the distances obtained from this algorithm is monotone. This is important for us in bounding the running time of our decremental data structures.

The following lemma shows that using the specific hopsets by [], we can maintain approximate single-source distances.
